represents the degree to which the evidence supports its negation. A natural way to introduce plausibility of a formula
\varphi
is to use the classical definition via the belief of the negation of
\varphi
. This definition is correct, because we know from Lemma
2.11
that since
\mathtt{bel}^{+}
is a general belief function on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
, then the map
\mathtt{pl}^{+}
defined as
\mathtt{pl}^{+}(\varphi)=1-\mathtt{bel}^{+}(\neg\varphi)
is a general plausibility function on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
. Similarly,
\mathtt{pl}^{-}(\varphi)=1-\mathtt{bel}^{-}(\neg\varphi)
is a general plausibility function on
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}^{op}
. Observe that in the case of strong contradictory belief in some proposition, it can happen that plausibility is strictly smaller than belief, contrary to the intuition understanding them as an upper and lower bound.

Formally, we can work with belnapian plausibility and take the pair
(\mathtt{pl}^{+}(\varphi),\mathtt{pl}^{-}(\varphi))
as the primary notion while belief would be a derived one, but this choice is less appealing from the point of view of an interpretation. In Example
4.1
, we will show that within this framework we can introduce a logic that allows us to characterise evidence in terms of how classical, incomplete or contradictory it is regarding a specific topic
\varphi
.
Indeed, in our framework, one can separate pieces of evidence into three categories:
classical (where the evidence for and evidence against add up exactly to
1
),
incomplete (the evidence for and evidence against add up to a number smaller than
1
), or contradictory (the evidence for and evidence against add up to a number greater than
1
).
Each of these gives us different signals.
In particular, classical information might be intuitively interpreted as indicative of us being on the right track in the investigation. I.e., if the information on
p
is classical, then the investigation into
p
can be deemed
satisfactory
. In this vein, incomplete information can be interpreted as us needing to
investigate
p
further
, while contradictory information on
p
shows us that our previous investigation was faulty, whence we need to
re-investigate once again
.

3.3.2
Combining belief and plausibility

The independence of positive and negative support which is the key idea of the Belnap-Dunn approach gives us more freedom in combining belief and plausibility than in the classical approach. While in the previous case, both positive and negative supports were represented by the same uncertainty measure, now we discuss the possibility of combining them. The idea behind the classical belief–plausibility relation is that we can see the plausibility of a proposition as a lack of support for its negation. This motivates our second choice for the representation of negative support of a proposition: it is not a (straightforward) support of its negation as in the previous case, but rather a lack of support of the proposition itself.

Formally, we consider a
\mathsf{DS}_{\mathtt{pl}}
model containing both belief and plausibility (in general they might be computed from different mass functions, so they are not mutually definable) and define the positive and negative support pair as
(\mathtt{bel}^{+}(\varphi),\mathtt{pl}^{-}(\varphi))=(\mathtt{bel}^{+}(\varphi),\mathtt{pl}^{+}(\neg\varphi))
.

3.3.3
Belief and plausibility as lower and upper bounds

In this section, we focus on the interpretation of belief and plausibility as a lower and an upper approximation of the probability of a formula. To do so, we have to consider
\mathsf{DS}_{\mathtt{pl}}
models
\mathscr{M}=(S,\mathcal{P}(S),\mathtt{bel},\mathtt{pl},v^{+},v^{-})
with both belief and plausibility introduced independently, and study the implications of the following property:

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}(Y)\leq\mathtt{pl}(Y)\text{ for every }Y\subseteq\mathcal{P}(S)

(16)

Within the framework of
\mathsf{BD}
logic, this immediately implies that one cannot define
\mathtt{pl}
via
\mathtt{bel}
without imposing strong constraints on the valuation of the
\mathsf{BD}
model. Therefore, here we are studying the meaning of having a general belief function and a general plausibility function generated by two different mass functions.
First, notice that for every
v^{+}
, (
16
) is equivalent to

\sum_{X\subseteq|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)\leq 1-\sum_{X\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X).

(17)

Indeed, from Theorem
2.9
and Lemma
2.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}(|p|^{+})=\sum_{X\subseteq|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{pl}(|p|^{+})=1-\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}(|\neg p|^{+})=1-\sum_{X\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X).

In addition, since
\mathtt{bel}
and
\mathtt{pl}
are respectively belief and plausibility functions, then we get the constraint

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}(|p|^{+})=\sum_{X\subseteq|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{X\not\subseteq|p|^{-}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\mathtt{pl}(|p|^{+}),

(18)

because

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}(|p|^{+})=\sum_{X\subseteq|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\mathtt{pl}(|p|^{+})=1-\sum_{X\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\in\mathcal{P}(S)}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)-\sum_{X\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|p|^{-}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X).

We consider the following two-dimensional interpretation for belief and plausibility of
\phi
respectively:
\mathsf{B}\phi=(\mathtt{bel}^{+}(\phi),\mathtt{bel}^{-}(\phi))
and

\mathsf{Pl}\phi=(\mathtt{pl}^{+}(\phi),\mathtt{pl}^{-}(\phi))
.

Interpretation of the mass functions
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
and
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}

Here,
\mathsf{B}(\phi)=(x,y)
is interpreted as follows:
x=\mathtt{bel}^{+}(\phi)
is how much the agent is persuaded that
\phi
is true based on the evidence, and
y=\mathtt{bel}^{-}(\phi)
is how much the agent is persuaded that
\phi
is false based on the evidence. Persuasion relies on a wide range of evidence: reliable scientific evidence, but also an emotional reaction to an argument. Therefore, one could ignore the contradictoriness of some evidence to strengthen one’s opinion and one can ignore scientific evidence due to some bias.
Hence, when computing
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
, the agent uses a weak standard for saying that a piece of evidence supports some statement and is influenced by its own bias: highly contradictory evidence can be considered to support a statement and reliable evidence can be ignored.

However, the agent uses a different standard of evidence to decide that a statement is not plausible. We have
\mathtt{pl}(|p|^{+})=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)
, therefore,
p
is considered plausible if there is very little strong evidence supporting
\neg p
. We interpret this as follows: the agent considers
p
plausible if they are not convinced that
\neg p
is the case. Conviction is built on ‘reliable’ evidence. The meaning of ‘reliable’ will depend on the context: in a court, it could be the kind of evidence accepted by the court, in science it could be detailed proofs that have been reviewed by experts…One can for instance believe that a mathematical statement is false because of some personal intuition based on experience, even if there is a verified proof of the statement which makes it not very plausible (but not impossible) that it is false. Indeed, with time, some proofs are found to be false.

We propose the following interpretation of the mass functions.

\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
is computed by asking the question ‘does the evidence persuade the agent?’, while
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is computed by asking the question ‘is the evidence considered convincing by some given authority?’ In order to get a better intuition on what is going on, let us have a look at some examples.

\textstyle{s_{0}\ :}
\textstyle{s_{1}\ :\ p}
\textstyle{s_{2}\ :\ \neg p}
\textstyle{s_{3}\ :\ p,\neg p}

Figure 3:
Canonical model over
\mathtt{Prop}=\{p\}
.

Example 3.3

(Strong belief in
p\wedge\neg p
)
.

Consider the set of variables
\mathtt{Prop}=\{p\}
. The canonical model is in Figure
3
.
Assume that
\mathtt{bel}(|p\wedge\neg p|^{+})=1
. Therefore,

\sum_{X\subseteq|p\wedge\neg p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)=\sum_{X\subseteq\{s_{3}\}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)=\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(\varnothing)+\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(\{s_{3}\})=1.

Since
\mathtt{bel}
is a belief function on
\mathcal{P}(S)
, we have
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(\varnothing)=0
, therefore
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(\{s_{3}\})=1
. This means that based on the available evidence, the agent is persuaded that
p\wedge\neg p
is the case. Notice that

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}(|p\wedge\neg p|^{+})

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|p\wedge\neg p|^{-}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|\neg(p\wedge\neg p)|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|\neg p\vee p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|\neg p|^{+}\cup|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|p|^{-}\cup|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)

Therefore, the condition that
\mathtt{bel}\leq\mathtt{pl}
implies that

\displaystyle 1=\sum_{X\not\subseteq|p|^{-}\cup|p|^{+}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\sum_{X\not\subseteq\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(X)=\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(S).

(19)

Therefore, evidence that is strongly persuasive considering
p\wedge\neg p
is inconclusive regarding the plausibility of either
p
or
\neg p
.

Example 3.4

(Weak belief in either
p
or
\neg p
)
.

We still consider the previous model.
Assume now that
\mathtt{bel}(|p\vee\neg p|^{+})=0
. Therefore
\sum_{X\subseteq\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(X)=0
and
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(\{s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\})=1
.
The condition that
\mathtt{bel}\leq\mathtt{pl}
does not constrain the value of
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}
in any way. Therefore, the available evidence might be convincing from the point of view of a given authority, however, it did not persuade the agent.

4
Two-layered logics for uncertainty measures

When reasoning about the uncertainty measures defined on sets of events, we can formalise the measure itself as a modal operator
\mathsf{M}
. The behaviour of
\mathsf{M}
can be defined in two ways. The first option is to treat it as a usual modality as done in, e.g.
[
14
]
; the second one is to use a ‘two-layered’ approach and apply
\mathsf{M}

only to the formulas describing events
and then reason with these formulas in another, so-called,
outer-layer
, logic (cf., e.g.,
[
23
,
29
,
12
,
3
]
). The main
syntactic
difference between these two approaches is that in the two-layered treatment of measure modalities,
they do not nest
.

At first glance, this seems as too much of a restriction. It is, however, justified. First, the decidability of two-layered logics is often easy to establish. Furthermore, the decision procedures allow for the extraction of the complexity evaluations of the logics and are, in addition, quite straightforward (cf., e.g.,
[
30
]
for the two-layered systems based on fuzzy logics and
[
23
]
for those dealing with linear inequalities) as one can utilise those for the outer-layer logics. On the other hand, the straightforward decision procedures for the modal logics that
do allow
for the nesting of
\mathsf{M}
seem more difficult to obtain. E.g.,
[
14
]
shows that the logic
\mathrm{CKL}
is decidable using filtration, however, this result is not easy to apply if one wants to establish the complexity of
\mathrm{CKL}
-satisfiability or validity.

Second, nested modalities are difficult to interpret in the natural language. While
\mathsf{M}p
can be understood as ‘
p
is probable’, ‘the agent believes that
p
is the case’, etc. depending on
\mathsf{M}
, and its value can be straightforwardly derived from the measure of the subset of the sample space where
p
is true, the interpretation of formulas such as
\mathsf{M}(p\wedge\mathsf{M}q)
is considerably less intuitive. Thus, in this paper, we are formalising paraconsistent reasoning about uncertainty using two-layered logics.

The two-layered formalisms are also divided into two groups depending on how they formalise statements such as ‘probability of
\phi
is twice as high as the probability of
\chi
’. There are two options. The first one is less formal and more intuitive. We allow numerical reasoning with inequalities directly on the outer layer. This is the way it was originally done in
[
23
]
. Another approach, more ‘puristic’ from a logical point of view, would be to use an outer-layer logic that can express addition and subtraction, e.g., Łukasiewicz logic. This approach originated in
[
29
]
and has been investigated further in an abstract algebraic manner, e.g., in
[
12
,
3
]
. Usually, the classical propositional logic is employed to describe the events, with an exception of a
\mathsf{BD}
-based logic for belief functions considered in
[
49
]
. We will present the two-layered logics that are based on both of the approaches mentioned above.

Before we proceed further, let us give a short note on the interpretation of atomic modal formulas. Throughout the section, we will use the following atomic modal formulas with
\phi\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
:
\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\phi)
,
\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\phi)
,
\mathsf{Pr}\phi
,
\mathsf{B}\phi
, and
\mathsf{Pl}\phi
. Their values range over
[0,1]
and are interpreted as the degrees of an agent’s certainty in
\phi
given by the corresponding measure. E.g.,
\mathtt{w}^{-}(\phi)\geqslant\frac{2}{3}
means that the probability measure of
|\phi|^{-}
is at least
\frac{2}{3}
(cf. Definition
4.1
for further details);
v^{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(\mathsf{Pl}\phi)=\frac{1}{4}
stands for ‘the plausibility of
|\phi|^{+}
is equal to
\frac{1}{4}
’ (cf. Definition
4.30
), etc.

4.1
Two-layered logics with inequalities

In this section, we present two-layered logics axiomatising reasoning with non-standard belief and plausibility functions that follow the approach of
[
23
]
. Namely, we employ
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
-formulas on the inner layer and linear inequalities on the outer one.

4.1.1
Logic for non-standard probabilities

Definition 4.1

(BD weight formulas and their semantics)
.

a
primitive weight formula
(PWF) is an expression of the form

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c

with
a_{i},c\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\phi_{i}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
. The left hand side of a PWF is called a
weight term
.
A
weight formula
is a Boolean combination of primitive weight formulas.

Let
\mathscr{M}=\langle W,v^{+},v^{-},\mu\rangle
be a probabilistic
\mathsf{BD}
model,
\mathtt{m}
, the mass function associated to
\mu
, and
\alpha
, a weight formula. The satisfaction relation
\mathscr{M}\models\alpha
is defined as follows
9
9

9

Observe that weights correspond to sums of masses of states where formulas are verified or falsified, i.e., to their
probabilities
.
.

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\phi)\geqslant c

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\sum\limits_{w\vDash^{\pm}\phi}\mathtt{m}(w)\geqslant c

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models{\sim}\alpha

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\mathscr{M}\not\models\alpha

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\mathscr{M}\models\alpha\text{ and }\mathscr{M}\models\alpha^{\prime}

Other connectives:
\vee
,
\supset
, etc. can be defined in a usual manner.

A weight formula is called
valid
iff it is satisfied on every model. We say that a set of weight formulas
\Xi

entails

\alpha
(
\Xi\vDash_{\mathtt{m}\mathsf{BD}}\alpha
) iff there is no model
\mathscr{M}
s.t.
\mathscr{M}\models\Xi
but
\mathscr{M}\not\models\alpha
.

Below, we give a calculus that proves weight formulas.

Definition 4.2

(Axioms for
\mathsf{BD}
weight formulas — calculus
\mathtt{w}\mathsf{BD}
)
.

\mathsf{CPL}

\alpha
s.t.
\mathsf{CPL}\vdash\alpha

MP

\dfrac{\alpha\quad\alpha\rightarrow\beta}{\beta}

\mathsf{ineq}

All instances of valid formulas about linear inequalities.

W1

\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\phi)\geqslant 0
,
\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\phi)\leqslant 1

W2

\mathtt{w}^{\mp}(\phi)=\mathtt{w}^{\pm}(\neg\phi)

W3

\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\phi_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}\phi_{j}\right)

W4

\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi)\leqslant\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{w}^{-}(\phi)\geqslant\mathtt{w}^{-}(\phi^{\prime})
for all
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
s.t.
\phi\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}\phi^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.3

(Completeness)
.

\mathtt{w}\mathsf{BD}\vdash\alpha
iff
\alpha
is valid.

Proof.

The soundness part can be obtained by verifying that all axioms hold on all probabilistic BD models. Indeed, we reason classically about the mass, and
W1
–
W4
are just the axioms for non-standard probabilities.

For the completeness part, we reason by contraposition. Assume that
\mathtt{w}\mathsf{BD}\nvdash{\sim}\alpha
. We show that
\alpha
is satisfiable. Since we reason with the weight formulas using
\mathsf{CPL}
, we can w.l.o.g. assume that
\alpha
is in
\mathsf{DNF}
. Moreover, a
\mathsf{DNF}
is satisfiable iff at least one of its clauses is. Thus, we can consider only weight formulas of the form
\alpha=\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}
with
\pi_{i}
’s being either PWFs or their negations. Notice however that a negation of a PWF is provably equivalent via
\mathsf{ineq}
to a PWF

\displaystyle{\sim}\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c\right)\dashv\vdash_{\mathtt{w}\mathsf{BD}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}-a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi_{i})>-c

(20)

Thus, we assume w.l.o.g. that each
\pi_{i}
is a PWF. Furthermore, using
W2
we can get rid of
\mathtt{w}^{-}
and work with formulas containing
\mathtt{w}^{+}
only.

Now, using
W3
,
W4
and the fact that each
\phi\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
has a unique representation as an
\mathsf{fDNF}
over a given set of literals (cf. Definition
2.4
) up to permutation of clauses and variables we conduct the following provably equivalent transformations.

First, take one
\pi_{k}
(
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
)

\displaystyle\pi_{k}=a_{1}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi_{1})+\ldots+a_{s}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\phi_{s})\geqslant c_{k}

(21)

and put
\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{j})
’s instead of
\phi_{j}
’s. We denote the transformed formula with
\pi^{\alpha}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\pi^{\alpha}_{k}=a_{1}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{1}))+\ldots+a_{s}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{s}))\geqslant c_{k}

(22)

Now, we use
W3
and
\mathsf{ineq}
to remove terms of the form
\mathtt{w}^{+}(\psi\vee\psi^{\prime})
. Namely, let

\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{j})=\bigvee\limits_{t=1}^{u}\mathsf{cl}_{t}

with
\mathsf{cl}_{t}
being a conjunction of literals. Then, by
W3
,
\mathsf{ineq}
, and propositional reasoning, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{j}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}(\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{j}))=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,u\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot a_{j}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}_{t}\right)

(23)

Using
W4
, we remove repeating literals from
a_{j}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}_{t}\right)
’s. After that, we also add the following formulas as conjuncts (here,
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}
’s are
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}_{t}
’s without repeating literals).

\displaystyle\left[\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,u\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)\leqslant 1\right]\wedge\left[\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,u\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)\geqslant 0\right]

(24)

Observe that (
24
) is provable from (
23
) by
\mathsf{ineq}
. We denote the resulting formula with
(\pi^{\alpha}_{k})^{\wedge}
.

Then, we set

\alpha^{\wedge}=\bigwedge\limits^{n}_{k=1}(\pi^{\alpha}_{k})^{\wedge}

Finally, for each
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}
and
\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
present in
\alpha^{\wedge}
s.t.
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\vdash_{\mathsf{FDE}}\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
, we add

\displaystyle\left[\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)\leqslant\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\right)\right]\wedge\left[\mathtt{w}^{-}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)\geqslant\mathtt{w}^{-}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\right)\right]

(25)

as new conjuncts (again, observe that they are axioms). We call the resulting formula
(\alpha^{\wedge})^{+}
. It is clear that
\mathtt{w}\mathsf{FDE}\vdash\alpha\leftrightarrow(\alpha^{\wedge})^{+}
since all our transformations preserved provable equivalence.

Now, observe that
(\alpha^{\wedge})^{+}
is satisfiable iff the system of inequalities obtained from its conjuncts by replacing
w^{\pm}(\psi)
’s with
x^{\pm}_{\psi}
’s has a solution (but if it does not, then
{\sim}(\alpha^{\wedge})^{+}\in\mathsf{ineq}
, whence
{\sim}\alpha\in\mathsf{ineq}
, contrary to our initial assumption). Indeed,
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}
’s correspond to states in a model, and the constraints on their masses are given in (
24
) and (
25
).

Now, we construct a model from the solution to the system of inequalities setting
W=\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha))
. For this, we need to translate weights into the mass assignments. First, we order
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}
’s w.r.t.
\vdash_{\mathsf{FDE}}
. Note that by the construction described in (
22
) and (
25
), there is the least element w.r.t. this order, namely, the conjunction of all literals generated by the
\mathsf{Var}(\alpha)
(in fact, every
\mathsf{fDNF}
contains such clause). Observe, further, that due to (
25
) it has the smallest positive weight.

Following the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 4]
, we define masses of states inductively, starting from
\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)
. We set
\mathtt{m}(\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha))=\mathtt{w}^{+}(\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha))
. As for the other states obtained from
\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}
’s, we define their masses as follows.

\displaystyle\mathtt{m}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)=\mathtt{w}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}\right)-\sum\limits_{\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\not\dashv~\vdash_{\mathsf{FDE}}\bigwedge\limits_{t\in J}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t}}\mathtt{m}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{t^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}}\mathsf{cl}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}\right)

(26)

If the sum of masses so acquired is still smaller than
1
, the remainder goes to the state that corresponds to the empty set of literals. All other states get mass zero. The measure can be then reconstructed from
\mathtt{m}
.
∎

4.1.2
Logic for general belief functions

Definition 4.4

(BD belief formulas and their semantics)
.

A
primitive belief formula
(PBF) is an expression of the following form

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c

with
a_{i},c\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\phi_{i}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
. The left hand side of a PBF is called a
belief term
. A
belief formula
is a Boolean combination of PBFs.

Let now
\mathscr{M}=\langle\mathcal{A},\mathtt{bel}_{1},\mathtt{bel}_{2}\rangle
be a De Morgan algebra with a pair belief functions such that
\mathtt{bel}_{i}(x)=\mathtt{bel}_{j}(\neg x)
(
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,
i\neq j
) for all
x\in\mathcal{A}
on it, and
\alpha
be a belief formula. We define the satisfaction relation
\mathscr{M}\models\alpha
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{bel}_{1}(v(\phi_{i}))\geqslant c

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{-}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{bel}_{2}(v(\phi_{i}))\geqslant c

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models{\sim}\alpha

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\mathscr{M}\not\models\alpha

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}\models\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ iff }\mathscr{M}\models\alpha\text{ and }\mathscr{M}\models\alpha^{\prime}

\alpha
is called valid iff it is satisfied on every
\mathscr{M}
.

Definition 4.5

(Axioms for
\mathsf{BD}
belief formulas — calculus
\mathtt{b}\mathsf{BD}
)
.

\mathsf{CPL}

\alpha
s.t.
\mathsf{CPL}\vdash\alpha

MP

\dfrac{\alpha\quad\alpha\rightarrow\beta}{\beta}

\mathsf{ineq}

All instances of valid formulas about linear inequalities.

B1

\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\phi)\geqslant 0
,
\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\phi)\leqslant 1

B2

\mathtt{b}^{\mp}(\phi)=\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\neg\phi)

B3

\mathtt{b}^{+}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\phi_{i}\right)\geqslant\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mathtt{b}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}\phi_{j}\right)

B4

\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi)\leqslant\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{b}^{-}(\phi)\geqslant\mathtt{b}^{-}(\phi^{\prime})
for all
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
s.t.
\phi\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}\phi^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\pi=\left[\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\phi_{i})\geqslant c\right]
be a PBF and let
X\supseteq\mathsf{Lit}(\pi)
. We call
\pi

X
-canonical
iff
\phi_{i}
is not equivalent to
\phi_{j}
for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
s.t.
i\neq j
, all occurring
\phi_{i}
’s are in fDNF w.r.t.
X
, and all occurrences of
\mathtt{b}^{\pm}
are positive.

Proposition 4.7
.

For any PBF
\pi
and for any
X\supseteq\mathsf{Lit}(\pi)
there exists a canonical PBF
\pi^{\mathsf{c}}
s.t.
\mathtt{b}\mathsf{BD}\vdash\pi\leftrightarrow\pi^{\mathsf{c}}
.

Proof.

Observe that we can use
\mathsf{ineq}
,
B2
, and
B4
to make a PBF canonical.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

\mathtt{b}\mathsf{BD}\vdash\alpha
iff
\alpha
is valid.

Proof.

To verify soundness, we just check that all axioms are valid. For the completeness part, we reason by contraposition. Assume that
\mathtt{b}\mathsf{BD}\nvdash{\sim}\alpha
. We show that
\alpha
is satisfiable.

We assume w.l.o.g. that
\alpha
is in DNF. Whence, we can reduce
\alpha
to a conjunction of PBFs (recall that because of (
20
) a negation of PBF is a PBF as well). Furthermore, using proposition
4.7
, we can further assume that all PBFs are
\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)
-canonical. Thus we set
\alpha\coloneqq\bigwedge\limits_{i=1}^{n}\pi^{\mathsf{c}}_{i}
with

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathsf{c}}_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{\pm}(\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi_{j}))\geqslant c_{i}

(27)

We add the following conjuncts to
\alpha
.

1.

\mathtt{b}^{+}(\mathsf{fDNF}_{\mathsf{Lit}(\alpha)}(\phi))\!\geqslant\!\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mathtt{b}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{k\in K}\mathsf{cl}_{k}\right)
for every
\phi
over
\mathsf{Var}(\alpha)
up to
\dashv\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}
10
10

10

Recall that
\mathsf{BD}
is tabular (locally finite), whence there are only finitely many pairwise non-equi-provable formulas over a given finite set of variables.
.

2.

\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi)\leqslant\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi^{\prime})
for each
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
added to
\alpha
on step
1
s.t.
\phi\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}\phi^{\prime}
.

3.

0\leqslant\mathtt{b}^{+}(\phi)\leqslant 1
for each
\phi
added to
\alpha
on the previous steps.

Observe that these conjuncts are axioms. Hence, the new formula (we dub it with
\alpha^{+}
) is provably equivalent to
\alpha
. Furthermore, it is clear that
\alpha^{+}
is just a system of linear equations which is satisfiable (otherwise,
{\sim}\alpha^{+}
is an instance of
\mathsf{ineq}
).

It remains to construct a model. For this, we take the Lindenbaum algebra generated by
\mathsf{Var}(\alpha)
, and define
\mathtt{b}^{+}([\phi])
using a solution for
\alpha^{+}
. Note that because of
2
,
\mathtt{b}^{+}
is defined for every element. Then define
\mathtt{b}^{-}([\phi])
as
\mathtt{b}^{+}([\neg\phi])
. Clearly,
\mathtt{b}^{+}
and
\mathtt{b}^{-}
are belief functions on the Lindenbaum algebra because items
1
,
2
, and
3
are exactly the belief functions axioms.
∎

4.2
Łukasiewicz logic and its two-dimensional expansions

In this part of Section
4
we will develop two-layered modal logics to reason about probabilities, and belief and plausibility functions. Before we do so, we concentrate on two
outer logics
of the two-layered framework, introduce their syntax, semantics, and prove their finite strong standard completeness.

As a starting point, we have chosen to use Łukasiewicz infinitely-valued logic (cf.
[
28
,
11
]
), which we introduce briefly together with its standard semantics on the real interval
[0,1]
. The choice is motivated by the fact that in the language of Łukasiewicz logic one can express the order and arithmetical operations, and consequently formulate the probability or belief function axioms. For the sake of translations between the linear inequalities-based formalism and the Łukasiewicz logic-based formalism, we consider Łukasiewicz logic extended with globalisation (Baaz delta) operator
\triangle
.

Definition 4.9

(Language and standard semantics of
Ł
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
)
.

The logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
has the following language
\mathscr{L}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
(
Ł
refers to the
\triangle
-free fragment):

\alpha\coloneqq\ p\mid\alpha\rightarrow\beta\mid{\sim}\alpha\mid\triangle\alpha,

with
p\in\mathtt{Prop}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
, and with additional connectives definable as

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\&\alpha

\displaystyle\alpha\oplus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}\alpha\rightarrow\beta

\displaystyle\alpha\ominus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\&\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\beta)

\displaystyle\bot

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(p\rightarrow p)

We interpret the language on the standard MV algebra
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}=([0,1],\rightarrow_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},{\sim_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}},\triangle_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}})
, with the following operations (we list some of the defined connectives as well for convenience)

\displaystyle a~\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{0,a+b-1\}

\displaystyle a~\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{1,1-a+b\}

\displaystyle a~\oplus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{1,a~+b\}

\displaystyle a~\ominus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{0,a~-b\}

\displaystyle a~\wedge b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{a,b\}

\displaystyle a~\vee b

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{a,b\}

\displaystyle\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a~

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}1,\text{ if }a~=1\\
0\ \ \text{else}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}a

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-a

The logic
Ł
(
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
) is semantically given as the
1
-preserving consequence relation over
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
in the corresponding language
11
11

11

This consequence is
infinitary

[
28
]
. Therefore one in general only obtains strong completeness for its finitary restriction.
.

The logic
Ł
can be axiomatised by the Łukasiewicz axioms in the language
\{\rightarrow,{\sim}\}
, or equivalently as an extension of the basic fuzzy logic BL with the involution axiom
[
28
]
.
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
is axiomatised by extending
Ł
with additional
\triangle
-axioms and
\triangle
-necessitation rule. Both the logics
Ł
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
are known to be
finitely
strongly standard complete w.r.t their standard semantics.
The axiomatisations, together with a proof of their finite strong standard completeness (FSSC), can be found e.g. in
[
28
]
or
[
11
]
.

We will expand the language of
Ł
, and extend the logics
Ł
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
in the following sections, to encompass a two-dimensional nature of positive / negative information reflected in the uncertainty measures developed in Subsections
3.1
and
3.3
. The axiomatisations of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
will therefore appear as part of our later definitions, and we will
comment on and refer to the particular places in the literature throughout the following section.

To encompass a two-dimensional nature of the reasoning semantically, we will use two different expansions of the
twist product
algebra
[0,1]^{\Join}
, i.e. twist product
12
12

12

In the context of Nelson’s paraconsistent logics such product construction has been called twist product of algebras
[
45
]
, or twist structures
[
36
, Chapter 8]
.
of the lattice
([0,1],\min,\max)
, as the algebra of values of the outer layer.

Definition 4.10

(Twist product
[0,1]^{\Join}
)
.

The twist product algebra
[0,1]^{\Join}
with the truth (vertical) order
\leq
and the information (horizontal) order
\leq_{i}
is defined as
[0,1]^{\Join}\coloneqq([0,1]\times[0,1]^{op},\wedge,\vee,\neg)
, where

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\wedge(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\wedge b_{1},a_{2}\vee b_{2})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\vee(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\vee b_{1},a_{2}\wedge b_{2})

\displaystyle\neg(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{2},a_{1})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\leq(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle\mbox{ iff }a_{1}\leq_{[0,1]}b_{1}\mbox{ and }b_{2}\leq_{[0,1]}a_{2}

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\leq_{i}(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle\mbox{ iff }a_{1}\leq_{[0,1]}b_{1}\mbox{ and }a_{2}\leq_{[0,1]}b_{2}

(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,1)
(1,1)

We will use different expansions of the twist product
[0,1]^{\Join}
with Łukasiewicz-derived connectives as the algebras (or to be precise, matrices) for the outer logic. There are some design choices to make, most importantly the following: (i) the choice of the set of designated values, and (ii) the choice how to interpret the additional connectives (mainly, the implication) in their second coordinate, i.e. choosing how they are to be negated with
\neg
, in other words, what type of information constitutes grounds for them to be refuted.

As for (i), we will use the following two natural choices: first, we can use the singleton
\{(1,0)\}
, i.e. the top of the underlying truth order as the only designated value. This entails that only formulas whose truth is absolutely supported
and
their falsity absolutely refuted are going to be valid. This in general yields a logic whose
(\wedge,\vee,\neg)
fragment coincides with the Exactly-true logic
\mathsf{ETL}

[
39
]
. Another choice is to put the filter
(1,1)^{\uparrow}=\{a\mid a_{1}=1\}
as the set of designated values. This entails that all formulas whose truth is absolutely supported are valid, so, the concept of validity only employs the first coordinate of their semantical value. This in general yields a logic whose
(\wedge,\vee,\neg)
fragment coincides with the logic
\mathsf{BD}
.

As for (ii), there are (at least) two natural ways of negating implication:
(a) a ’de Morgan’ way, dualising implication with a
co-implication
(which in case of Łukasiewicz logic is
\ominus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
) as

\neg(a\rightarrow b)\coloneqq(\neg b\ominus\neg a),

and (b) a ’Nelson’s way, combining positive and negative semantical values with a suitable conjunction as

\neg(a\rightarrowtriangle b)\coloneqq(a\ \&\ \neg b).

The former mimics one of the options on how to negate implications listed in
[
46
]
,
the latter is inspired directly by how implication is negated in Nelson’s paraconsistent logic
\mathsf{N}4

[
35
]
, and it yields an implication whose associated equivalence connective is not congruential, and it is therefore referred to as a weak implication (and we will use a different symbol to denote it).

Logics of both kinds have been considered in
[
8
]
, namely the two logics

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,0)}(\rightarrow)

and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,1)^{\uparrow}}(\rightarrowtriangle)
(the lower index pointing to the set of designated values). There, constraint tableaux calculi were provided to decide their sets of tautologies and both logics were shown to be coNP-complete. The logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,0)}(\rightarrow)
has previously been introduced and proven complete in
[
7
]
under the name
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}_{\neg}
.

In this paper, we employ the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,0)}(\rightarrow)
, extended with a
\triangle
operator, to reason about probabilities or belief functions over the logic
\mathsf{BD}
. We will refer to this logic as a Belnapian extension of
Ł
, and describe it in detail in the following Subsection
4.2.1
. Next, we will employ the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,1)^{\uparrow}}(\rightarrowtriangle)
to reason about belief and plausibility as described in the Subsection
3.3.2
. We will refer to this logic as a Nelson-style extension of
Ł
, and describe it in detail in the Subsection
4.2.2
.

4.2.1
A Belnapian extension of Łukasiewicz logic

In this paper, we first opt to use the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,0)}(\rightarrow)
of
[
8
]
, expanded with a globalisation
\triangle
operator. We will skip mentioning the language in the index throughout this subsection for simplicity of notation, and use
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
to refer to the logic.

Definition 4.11

(Language and semantics of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
)
.

The logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
has the following language
\mathscr{L}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}
:

\alpha\coloneqq\ p\mid\alpha\rightarrow\beta\mid{\sim}\alpha\mid\triangle\alpha\mid\neg\alpha,

with
p\in\mathtt{Prop}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
, and with additional connectives definable as

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\wedge\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\&\alpha

\displaystyle\alpha\oplus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}\alpha\rightarrow\beta

\displaystyle\alpha\ominus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\&\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\beta)

\displaystyle\bot

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(p\rightarrow p)

To interpret the language, we expand the twist product

[0,1]^{\Join}

with the following operations derived from the standard MV algebra (we list the semantics for some of the derived connectives as well for convenience):

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\rightarrow(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},b_{2}\ominus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\ominus(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\ominus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},b_{2}\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\&(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},a_{2}\oplus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{2})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\oplus(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\oplus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},a_{2}\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{2})

\displaystyle{\sim}(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=(\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{1},\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{2})

\displaystyle\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=(\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{1},{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}a_{2})

\displaystyle\neg(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{2},a_{1})

(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,1)
(1,1)

We put the singleton
\{(1,0)\}
to be the set of designated values and denote the resulting matrix
([0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\{(1,0)\})
. Observe, that we can understand the
\neg
-negation geometrically as the symmetry along the horizontal line, while the
{\sim}
-negation
{\sim}(x,y)=(1-y,1-x)
geometrically denotes the symmetry along the middle point
(0.5,0.5)
.

The semantical consequence of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
is defined as
\{(1,0)\}
preservation over all valuations
v:\mathtt{Prop}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\to[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
(extended to the whole language
\mathscr{L}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}
as expected):

\Gamma\vDash_{[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}\alpha\ \ \mbox{ iff }\ \ \forall v(v[\Gamma]\subseteq\{(1,0)\}\Rightarrow v(\alpha)=(1,0)).

Convention 4.1
.

Given a valuation
v
s.t.
v(\phi)=(x,y)
, we will further write
v_{1}(\phi)=x
and
v_{2}(\phi)=y
when dealing with the coordinates of
v
.

Remark 4.1
.

The matrix
([0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\{(1,0)\})
can be equivalently seen as the product MV algebra
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\times[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{op}
, where
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
is the standard MV algebra and
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{op}
is its dual: the operations and order of the standard MV algebra
([0,1],\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\&_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\ominus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\oplus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},{\sim_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}})
, are turned upside down and dualized in the dual algebra as
([0,1]^{op},\vee,\wedge,\ominus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\oplus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\rightarrow_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\&_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},{\sim_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}})
. The natural dual of the globalisation Baaz delta operator
\triangle_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
(”definitely true”) is then the operator
{\sim_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}\triangle_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}{\sim_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
, which results in
0
if its argument is
0
, and in
1
otherwise.

Remark 4.2

(The two-dimensional delta operators)
.

Observe that
\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})
can only result in the four values
(1,0),(1,1),(0,0),(0,1)
in
[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
:
\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})=(1,0)
if and only if
((a_{1},a_{2})=(1,0))
,
\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})=(1,1)
if and only if
a_{1}=1,a_{2}\neq 0
,
\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})=(0,0)
if and only if
a_{1}\neq 1,a_{2}=0
, and
\triangle(a_{1},a_{2})=(0,1)
else. Defining the following operator

{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\coloneqq\triangle\alpha\wedge{\sim}\neg\triangle\alpha,

we obtain the
(1,0)
-detecting operator in
[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
: it has value
(1,0)
iff the argument has the value
(1,0)
, and is
(0,1)
everywhere else.

It is well known that the Łukasiewicz language expanded with
\triangle
can define the Gödel implication (cf.
[
10
, CH.I, 2.2.1]
) as
\alpha\rightarrow_{G}\beta\coloneqq\triangle(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\vee\beta
. Similarly, co-implication can be defined as
\beta\Yleft_{G}\alpha\coloneqq\beta\wedge{\sim}\triangle(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)
. Thus in
[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
,

\displaystyle\triangle(a\rightarrow b)\vee b

\displaystyle=(\triangle(a_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}b_{1})\vee b_{1},{\sim}\triangle{\sim}(b_{2}\ominus_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}a_{2})\wedge b_{2})

\displaystyle=(\triangle(a_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}b_{1})\vee b_{1},{\sim}\triangle(b_{2}\rightarrow a_{2})\wedge b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\rightarrow_{G}b_{1},b_{2}\Yleft_{G}a_{2})

defines the implication (and consequently also other connectives) of the two-dimensional bi-Gödel logic
\mathsf{G}_{(1,0)}^{2}(\rightarrow)
introduced in
[
8
]

to reason about comparative uncertainty.

It is worth observing, that due to
{\triangle^{\top}}
, we can express both the order and the strict order of the
[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
: First,
a\rightarrow b
is designated if and only if
a\leq b
, thus
{\triangle^{\top}}(a\rightarrow b)
is
(1,0)
if
a\leq b
, and it is
(0,1)
otherwise. Next, the formula
{\triangle^{\top}}(a\rightarrow b)\wedge{\sim}{\triangle^{\top}}(b\rightarrow a)
is
(1,0)
if
a<b
, and it is
(0,1)
otherwise.

Definition 4.12

(Axiomatisation of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
)
.

The logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
is axiomatised by the following axioms and rules:

(w)

\alpha\rightarrow(\beta\rightarrow\alpha)

(
\triangle
1)

\triangle\alpha\vee{\sim}\triangle\alpha

(sf)

(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\gamma))

(
\triangle
2)

\triangle\alpha\rightarrow\alpha

(waj)

((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow((\beta\rightarrow\alpha)\rightarrow\alpha)

(
\triangle
3)

\triangle\alpha\rightarrow\triangle\triangle\alpha

(co)

({\sim}\beta\rightarrow{\sim}\alpha)\rightarrow(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)

(
\triangle
4)

\triangle(\alpha\vee\beta)\rightarrow\triangle\alpha\vee\triangle\beta

(dn
\neg
)

\neg\neg\alpha\leftrightarrow\alpha

(
\triangle
5)

\triangle(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\triangle\alpha\rightarrow\triangle\beta

(
\neg{\sim}
)

\neg{\sim}\alpha\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg\alpha

(
\neg\triangle
)

\neg\triangle\alpha\leftrightarrow{\sim}\triangle{\sim}\neg\alpha

(K
{\sim}\neg
)

({\sim}\neg\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\neg\beta)\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)

(Nec)

\alpha\vdash\triangle\alpha

(
\vee
)

\alpha\vee\beta\leftrightarrow((\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\rightarrow\beta)

(Conf)

\alpha\vdash{\sim}\neg\alpha

(MP)

\alpha,\alpha\rightarrow\beta\vdash\beta

The axioms of weakening, suffixing, Wajsberg’s axiom, and (converse) contraposition (w, sf, waj, co) together with the rule (MP) completely axiomatise Łukasiewicz logic in the language
\{\rightarrow,{\sim}\}
(cf.
[
28
]
). Axiom (
\vee
) is the definition of disjunction, which we need to formulate two of the the
\triangle
axioms.

The delta axioms (
\triangle
1–5) and the (Nec) rule above, except the additional (
\neg\triangle
) axiom, present the usual complete axiomatisation of Baaz Delta operator over Łukasiewicz logic (cf.
[
28
, Def. 2.4.5]
or
[
10
, CH I,2.2.1]
).

First, observe that the
\neg
negations can provably be pushed to the atomic formulas, and we can thus consider formulas, up to provable equivalence, in a
negation normal form (NNF)
, i.e. formulas built using
\{\rightarrow,\sim,\triangle\}
from
literals
of the form
p,\neg p
:

Lemma 4.13

(NNF)
.

For each formula
\alpha
in
\mathscr{L}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}
there is a formula in
\neg
-negation normal form
\alpha^{\star}
such that

\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\alpha\leftrightarrow\alpha^{\star}.

Proof.

We have the axiom (
\neg\triangle
), and we have
\neg{\sim}\alpha\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg\alpha
and
\neg(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\leftrightarrow{\sim}{\sim}\neg(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)\leftrightarrow{\sim}({\sim}\neg\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\neg\beta)
provable. A procedure can therefore be defined which turns each
\alpha
into
\alpha^{\star}
in nnf, so that we can prove, by induction, that
({\sim}\alpha)^{\star}\leftrightarrow{\sim}\alpha^{\star}
,
(\triangle\alpha)^{\star}\leftrightarrow\triangle\alpha^{\star}
, and
(\alpha\rightarrow\beta)^{\star}\leftrightarrow{\sim}({\sim}\alpha^{\star}\rightarrow{\sim}\beta^{\star})
.
∎

Remark 4.3

(The conflation)
.

The composition of the two negations
{\sim}\neg
works semantically as follows:
{\sim}\neg(a_{1},a_{2})=(1-a_{2},1-a_{1})
, and we can geometrically understand it on the
[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
algebra as the symmetry along the vertical line. Such operation is often referred to as
conflation
(cf., e.g.
[
24
,
37
]
), and it is a form of an involutive information negation (indeed, we have provably
{\sim}\neg{\sim}\neg\alpha\leftrightarrow\alpha
).

One can easily prove in the calculus above that conflation distributes with all the Łukasiewicz connectives
\{\rightarrow,{\sim},\triangle,\&,\wedge,\vee,\oplus,\ominus\}
(it suffices to use
(K{\sim}\neg,\neg{\sim})
and
(\neg\triangle)
axioms, together with the definitions of the derived connectives).

Remark 4.4

(The
\triangle
and
{\triangle^{\top}}
operators)
.

Observe that the
\triangle
operator has some useful additional properties (we list only those we will need later: proofs of most of them in
Ł
can be found e.g., in
[
28
, Lemma 2.4.11]
, and they can also similarly be proven in the calculus above):

\displaystyle\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\triangle\alpha\leftrightarrow\triangle(\alpha\&\alpha)\leftrightarrow\triangle\alpha\&\triangle\alpha

\displaystyle\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\triangle(\alpha\&\beta)\leftrightarrow\triangle\alpha\&\triangle\beta\leftrightarrow\triangle(\alpha\wedge\beta)

\displaystyle\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}(\triangle\alpha\rightarrow(\triangle\alpha\rightarrow\beta))\rightarrow(\triangle\alpha\rightarrow\beta)

The last formula is a form of the contraction principle for
\triangle
-formulas, and its provability follows immediately from the
(\triangle 1)
and
(\vee)
axioms.

Observing that
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\coloneqq\triangle\alpha\wedge{\sim}\neg\triangle\alpha
and thus, by the above principles and conflation distribution over
\triangle
, provably
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\leftrightarrow\triangle(\alpha\wedge{\sim}\neg\alpha)
, one can consequently prove all the above principles for
{\triangle^{\top}}
-formulas as well. Namely, the contraction principle
({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta))\rightarrow({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta)
is provable.
Moreover, the
(\triangle 1-2,\triangle 4-5)
axioms and the (Nec) rule with
\triangle
replaced by
{\triangle^{\top}}
remain both sound and derivable. It is worth noting that it is
not
the case for the remaining
(\triangle 3)
axiom. As an additional principle we can prove that
{\triangle^{\top}}
-formulas are fixpoints of conflation:
{\sim}\neg{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\leftrightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha
.

Applying the insights remarked on above, we can prove that the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
has the following
{\triangle^{\top}}
-deduction theorem (cf.
[
28
, Th. 2.4.14]
or
[
10
, Ch.I, Th. 2.2.1]
):

Lemma 4.14

(DT
{\triangle^{\top}}
)
.

\Gamma,\alpha\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\beta\ \ \mbox{iff}\ \ \Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta.

Proof.

The proof of the theorem is rather standard, by induction on the length
n
of the proof of
\Gamma,\alpha\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\beta
.

n=1
:

In this case
\beta
is either an instance of an axiom, or an assumption form
\Gamma
, or
\alpha=\beta
. In the first two cases, the result follows by the (w) axiom. The remaining case uses that
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\alpha
is provable.

n>1
:

We assume that for all shorter proofs the results holds, and let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{n},\beta
be a proof of
\beta
from
\Gamma\cup\{\alpha\}
. We distinguish three cases:

(Conf)

Let
\beta\coloneqq{\sim}\neg\gamma_{n}
be obtained by the conflation rule. By the induction hypothesis,
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\gamma_{n}
is provable. By an application of (Conf), (K
{\sim}\neg
) and (MP) we obtain
{\sim}\neg{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\neg\gamma_{n}
. By
{\sim}\neg{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\leftrightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha
(see Remark
4.4
) it follows that

{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\neg\gamma_{n}
as desired.

(Nec)

Let
\beta\coloneqq\triangle\gamma_{n}
be obtained by the necessitation rule. By the induction hypothesis,
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\gamma_{n}
is provable. By an application of (Nec), using provability of
{\triangle^{\top}}({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\gamma_{n})\rightarrow({\triangle^{\top}}{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\gamma_{n})
, and (MP) we obtain
{\triangle^{\top}}{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\gamma_{n}
. Using provability of
{\triangle^{\top}}{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\leftrightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha
(see Remark
4.4
) we conclude
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow{\triangle^{\top}}\gamma_{n}
as desired.

(MP)

Let
m<n
and let
\gamma_{m},\gamma_{n}=\gamma_{m}\rightarrow\beta/\beta
be an instance of modus ponens. By the induction hypothesis we have
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\gamma_{m}
and
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow(\gamma_{m}\rightarrow\beta)
provable. From the latter we have also
\gamma_{m}\rightarrow({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta)
provable. Thus, by (suf) and (MP) we obtain
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow({\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta)
. By the contraction principle of Remark
4.4
we finally obtain
{\triangle^{\top}}\alpha\rightarrow\beta
as desired.

∎

We proceed to prove finite strong standard completeness (FSSC for short) of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
, i.e. strong completeness of the finite consequence of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
w.r.t. the matrix
([0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}},\{(1,0)\})
.
We do so by reducing the provability in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
to that in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
, which itself is known to be finite strong standard complete, i.e. finite strong complete w.r.t. the standard MV algebra
[0,1]_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
(see e.g.
[
28
, Th. 3.2.14]
):

Lemma 4.15

(Reducing
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
)
.

For any
finite
set of formulas
\Gamma,\alpha
in a NNF,

\Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\alpha\
iff

for some finite
\Sigma:\ \boxdot\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle}\alpha,

where
{\sim}\neg\Gamma\coloneqq\{{\sim}\neg\gamma\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
and
\boxdot\Gamma\coloneqq{\sim}\neg\Gamma\cup\Gamma
, and
\Sigma
contains instances of
\neg
-axioms.

Proof.

The right-left direction is almost trivial:
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
is a subsystem of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
, and all the axioms in
\Sigma
are provable in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
, and, thanks to the
{\sim}\neg
-rule,
\Sigma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\boxdot\gamma
for each
\gamma\in\Sigma
.

For the other direction, we proceed in a few steps. First, we denote by
\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2-}}
provability in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}

without
the
{\sim}\neg
-rule. By routine induction on proofs (and using that
{\sim}\neg
distributes from/to implications, negations, and
\triangle
), we can see that

\Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\alpha\
iff

\boxdot\Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2-}}\alpha.

Then we can list all the instances of
\neg
-axioms in the proof above in
\Sigma
, and obtain:

\boxdot\Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2-}}\alpha\
iff

\boxdot\Gamma,\Sigma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle}\alpha.

First, note that we can include in
\Sigma
all instances of
\neg
-axioms for all subformulas of
\Gamma,\alpha
as well and still keep the Lemma valid. This will come in handy in the following completeness proof. Second, we stress that in the final proof
\boxdot\Sigma,\Sigma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle}\alpha
in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
, we still use the language of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
, where formulas starting with
\neg
are seen from the point of view of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
as atomic.
∎

Lemma
4.15
provides a translation of provability in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
to provability in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
, and allows us in particular to observe that the extension of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
by
\neg
is conservative. Now, using finite strong standard completeness of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
, we can see that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
is finitely strongly standard complete:

Lemma 4.16

(FSSC)
.

For a
finite
set of formulas
\Gamma
,

\Gamma\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\alpha
iff

\Gamma\vDash_{[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}\alpha.

Proof.

The left-right direction expresses soundness and consists of checking that all the axioms are valid and all the rules sound. We only do some cases:

First the
{\sim}\neg
-rule: assume that
v
is given and
v(\alpha)=(1,0)
. Then

v({\sim}\neg\alpha)={\sim}\neg(1,0)={\sim}(0,1)=(1,0).

Next, for any
v
,

\displaystyle v({\sim}\neg(\alpha\rightarrow\beta))

\displaystyle={\sim}\neg(v(\alpha)\rightarrow v(\beta))

\displaystyle={\sim}\neg(v_{1}(\alpha)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\beta),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}(v_{2}(\beta)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{2}(\alpha)))

\displaystyle=((v_{2}(\beta)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{2}(\alpha)),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}(v_{1}(\alpha)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\beta))),

and

\displaystyle v({\sim}\neg\alpha\rightarrow{\sim}\neg\beta)

\displaystyle={\sim}\neg v(\alpha)\rightarrow{\sim}\neg v(\beta)

\displaystyle=({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{1}(\alpha))\rightarrow({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\beta),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{1}(\beta))

\displaystyle=({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\beta),v_{1}(\alpha)\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{1}(\beta))

\displaystyle=((v_{2}(\beta)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{2}(\alpha)),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}(v_{1}(\alpha)\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\beta))).

Next, for any
v
,

v(\neg{\sim}\alpha)=\neg{\sim}v(\alpha)=({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{1}(\alpha))={\sim}\neg v(\alpha)=v({\sim}\neg\alpha).

Last, for any
v
,

\displaystyle v(\neg\triangle\alpha)

\displaystyle=\neg(v(\triangle\alpha))=\neg(\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\alpha),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha),\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\alpha))

\displaystyle={\sim}(\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}\triangle_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}v_{1}(\alpha))

\displaystyle={\sim}\triangle({\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{2}(\alpha),{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}v_{1}(\alpha))

\displaystyle={\sim}\triangle{\sim}\neg\alpha.

We leave the rest of the proof of soundness for the reader.

For the right-left direction, let us assume that
\Gamma\nvdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}\alpha
. Then for some finite
\Sigma
containing instances of
\neg
-axioms (in particular those for subformulas of
\Sigma,\alpha
), we have
\boxdot\Sigma,\Sigma\nvdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle}\alpha.
Because
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
is finitely standard complete, there is an evaluation
v:\mathrm{At}\to[0,1]_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
sending all formulas in
\boxdot\Sigma,\Sigma
to
1
, while
v(\alpha)<1
. Here,
\mathrm{At}
contains literals from
\Sigma,\alpha
of the form
p,\neg p
, and atoms and formulas of the form
\neg\sigma
from
\boxdot\Sigma,\Sigma
. We define
v^{\prime}:\mathtt{Prop}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\to[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
by

v^{\prime}(p)=(v(p),v(\neg p)).

Let us denote
\beta^{\neg}
the NNF of
\neg\beta
(i.e.
(\neg\beta)^{\star}
).
We can then prove, by routine induction, that for each formula
\beta
a subformula of
\Sigma,\alpha
, we have

v^{\prime}(\beta)=(v(\beta),v(\beta^{\neg})).

To do so, we use the fact that
v[\Sigma]\subseteq\{1\}
, and
\Sigma
contains all instances of
\neg
-axioms for all subformulas of
\Sigma,\alpha
, and, subsequently,
\Sigma
ensures that
v(\neg\beta)=v(\beta^{\neg})
.

We now immediately see that
v^{\prime}(\alpha)<(1,0)
, because
v(\alpha)<1
.
To prove that indeed
v^{\prime}[\Gamma]\subseteq\{(1,0)\}
, we use the fact that
v[\boxdot\Gamma]\subseteq\{1\}
: as for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
v({\sim}\neg\gamma)=1
,
v(\neg\gamma)=v(\gamma^{\neg})=0
.
For the latter, we again need to use the fact that
v[\Sigma]\subseteq\{1\}
, and
\Sigma
contains all instances of
\neg
-axioms for all subformulas of
\Gamma
, as they prove, by means of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}\triangle
, that
\neg\gamma\leftrightarrow\gamma^{\neg}
, and
v
has to respect that.
Now we conclude, that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
v^{\prime}(\gamma)=(v(\gamma),v(\gamma^{\neg}))=(1,0)
.
∎

Remark 4.5

(Changing the designated values)
.

Changing the set of designated values in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
to the filter
(1,1)^{\uparrow}
yields the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,1)^{\uparrow}}(\rightarrow)
of
[
8
]
, expanded with
\triangle
. It can be axiomatised by removing the conflation rule (Conf) from the axiomatisation of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
provided in Definition
4.12
. Its
(\wedge,\vee,\neg)
fragment coincides with
\mathsf{BD}
, while the
(\wedge,\vee,\neg)
fragment of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
coincides with
\mathsf{ETL}
. We will not use this logic in this paper, however, it can be proved finitely strongly complete by similar manner as
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
.

Example 4.1

(What
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
can express)
.

Recall first that, semantically,
\neg
is the symmetry along the horizontal,
\sim
is the symmetry along the middle point, and the conflation
{\sim}\neg
is the symmetry along the vertical line.
Let now
v_{1}(p)
quantify the evidence for
p
and
v_{2}(p)
quantify the evidence against
p
. As discussed in Section
3.3.1
, given any statement
p
, we can determine whether the evidence we have about it is classical, incomplete, or contradictory:

The formula
\neg p\leftrightarrow{\sim}p
, or equivalently
p\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg p
, defines the vertical line: for each valuation
v
,
v(\neg p\leftrightarrow{\sim}p)=(1,0)
if and only if
v_{1}(p)=1-v_{2}(p)
. In a sense,
p\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg p
measures how close our information about
p
is to the non-controversial classical case. Moreover, from the semantics of
{\triangle^{\top}}
, it is clear that we can test whether the information is classical by

\displaystyle v({\triangle^{\top}}(p\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg p))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(1,0)&\text{ if }v_{1}(p)=1-v_{2}(p)\\
(0,1)&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(28)

(
28
) is a useful tool. Assume, we have some finite set of statements
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
. Then, using finite strong completeness of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
, we can check whether those entail classicality of
p
by establishing the following inference

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\vdash_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}}{\triangle^{\top}}(p\leftrightarrow{\sim}\neg p).

Unfortunately,
p\rightarrow{\sim}\neg p
proves
{\sim}\neg p\rightarrow p
, and thus we cannot really distinguish the left-hand triangle (incompleteness) from the right-hand triangle (contradictoriness) using these implications. However,
if
p
is
not
classical, we can establish whether it is contradictory or incomplete for example as follows:

\displaystyle v_{1}(\triangle(p\rightarrow{\sim}\neg p))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ iff }$p$\text{ is incomplete}\\
0&\text{ iff }$p$\text{ is contradictory}\end{cases}

(29)

4.2.2
A Nelson-style extension of Łukasiewicz logic

For part of reasoning about belief functions and plausibilities, we will employ the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,1)^{\uparrow}}(\rightarrowtriangle)
of
[
8
]
. The interpretation of implication in this logic takes a direct inspiration from Nelson’s paraconsistent logic.
We will again skip mentioning the language in the index throughout this subsection and use simply
\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
to refer to the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}_{(1,1)^{\uparrow}}(\rightarrowtriangle)
.

Definition 4.17

(Language and semantics of
\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
)
.

The logic
\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
has the following language
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
:

\alpha\coloneqq\ p\mid\alpha\wedge\beta\mid\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta\mid{\sim}\alpha\mid\neg\alpha,

with
p\in\mathtt{Prop}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}
. We call the
\rightarrowtriangle
connective the weak implication. Additional connectives are definable as follows (where
\Rightarrow,\Leftrightarrow
are called strong implication and strong equivalence):

\displaystyle\alpha\&\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle{\sim}\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\Rightarrow\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta)\wedge(\neg\beta\rightarrowtriangle\neg\alpha)

\displaystyle\alpha\leftrightarrowtriangle\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta)\wedge(\beta\rightarrowtriangle\alpha)

\displaystyle\alpha\Leftrightarrow\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\alpha\leftrightarrowtriangle\beta)\wedge(\neg\alpha\leftrightarrowtriangle\neg\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\oplus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta

\displaystyle\alpha\ominus\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta)

\displaystyle\alpha\vee\beta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\neg(\neg\alpha\wedge\neg\beta)

\displaystyle\bot

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\sim}(p\rightarrow p)

To interpret the language, we expand the bilattice
[0,1]^{\Join}
with the following operations, derived again from the standard MV algebra (we spell out the semantics of some of the defined connectives for convenience):

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\rightarrowtriangle(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},a_{1}\&_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}b_{2})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\&(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1},a_{1}\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}{\sim}b_{1})

\displaystyle{\sim}(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=({\sim}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle\neg(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{2},a_{1})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\oplus(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\oplus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{2},{\sim_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}a_{1}\&_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{2})

\displaystyle(a_{1},a_{2})\ominus(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\ominus_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}a_{2},a_{1}\rightarrow_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}b_{1})

(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,1)
(1,1)

We put the filter
(1,1)^{\uparrow}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\mid x=1\}
to be the set of designated values, and denote the resulting matrix
([0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}},(1,1)^{\uparrow})
. Observe, that
{\sim}
now behaves differently than in the logic
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}^{2}
: namely, its value is always on the classical vertical line. The semantical consequence of
\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}
is defined as
(1,1)^{\uparrow}
preservation over all valuation in this algebra (where
v(\alpha)=(v_{1}(\alpha),v_{2}(\alpha))
):

\Gamma\vDash_{[0,1]^{\Join}_{\mathsf{N}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\L}}{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize\L}}{\mbox{\rm\tiny\L}}}}}\alpha\ \ \mbox{ iff }\ \ \forall v(v_{1}[\Gamma]\subseteq\{1\}\Rightarrow v_{1}(\alpha)=1).

Remark 4.6

(Strong and weak implication)
.

The connective
\rightarrowtriangle
is called the weak implication. If
v(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta)
is designated, then we only know that
v_{1}(\alpha)\leq v_{1}(\beta)
.
The derived weak equivalence
\alpha\leftrightarrowtriangle\beta\coloneqq(\alpha\rightarrowtriangle\beta)\wedge(\beta\rightarrowtriangle\alpha)
