\mu(A\cup B)=\mu(A)+\mu(B)-\mu(A\cap B)
for every
A,B\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. Then, we have:

\mu\left(\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\},J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mu\left(\bigcap\limits_{j\in J}R_{i}\right).

(30)

Theorem
2.9

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a finite lattice and
\mathtt{bel}:{\cal{L}}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a monotone function. Let further,
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}:{\cal{L}}\rightarrow[0,1]
be the Möbius transform of
\mathtt{bel}
. Then,
\mathtt{bel}
is a general belief function iff
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
is a general mass function.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a finite bounded lattice, then,
\mathtt{bel}
is a belief function iff
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
is a mass function.

In addition, if
\mathtt{bel}
is a (general) belief function, we have for every
x\in\mathcal{L}
,

\mathtt{bel}(x)=\sum\limits_{y\leq x}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}(y).

(31)

We call
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
the (general) mass function associated to
\mathtt{bel}
.

Proof.

The statement “
\mathtt{bel}
is a belief function iff
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
is a mass function”
follows from
[
49
, Theorem 2.8]
that states that
f
is weakly totally monotone iff
g
is non-negative. It is immediate to prove that
g
is indeed a mass function.

For the statement “
\mathtt{bel}
is a general belief function iff
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{bel}}
is a general mass function”.
The proof is similar to the proof of
Theorem
2.9
.
The differences are the following:
\bot
and
\top
are not in the signature of the lattice
{\cal{L}}
, we do not require the lattice to be distributive, and
f
is not required to be a belief function, therefore
g
is not necessarily a mass function.

Let
a\in{\cal{L}}
. First, we show that
g(a)\geq 0
.
Notice that, since
f(a)=\sum_{b\leq a}g(b)
, we have
g(a)=f(a)-\sum_{b<a}g(b)
. Let
A=\{x\in{\cal{L}}\mid x<a\}
. Recall that
A=\bigcup_{b<a}\downarrow b
, where
\downarrow b=\{x\in{\cal{L}}\mid x\leq b\}
.
Let
\mu:\mathcal{P}({\cal{L}})\rightarrow[0,1]
be such that
\mu(A)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in A}g(x)
. Notice that
\mu
is additive.
The following chain of equalities holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{b<a}g(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in A}g(x)

\displaystyle=\mu(A)

(definition of
\mu
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq A,J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mu\left(\bigcap_{b\in J}\downarrow b\right)

(
A=\bigcup_{b<a}\downarrow b
and
\mu
satisfies (
30
))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq A,J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}\left(\sum_{x\in\bigcap_{b\in J}\downarrow b}g(x)\right)

(definition of
\mu
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq A,J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}\left(\sum_{x\in\downarrow{\bigwedge\{x\mid x\in J\}}}g(x)\right)

(
\downarrow\!a~\cap\downarrow\!b=\downarrow\!(a\wedge b)
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{J\subseteq A,J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}f\left(\bigwedge\limits_{x\in J}x\right)

(definition of
f
and
\downarrow
)

Notice that, if there is no element smaller than
a
, we have
f(a)=g(a)
and
g(a)\geq 0
(because
f
is positive). Otherwise, the following chain of inequalities follows from the equality above and because
f
is monotone and weakly totally monotone.

f(a)\geq f\left(\bigvee_{b<a}b\right)\geq\sum\limits_{J\subseteq A,J\neq\varnothing}(-1)^{|J|+1}f\left(\bigwedge\limits_{x\in J}x\right)=\sum_{b<a}\mathtt{m}(b).

Therefore,
g(a)=f(a)-\sum_{b<a}g(b)\geq 0
as required.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is a finite lattice, it has a unique maximal element
t
. Since
g
is the Möbius transform of
f
, we have
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{L}}g(x)=\sum_{x\leq t}g(x)=f(t)
. Therefore, since
g
is non-negative,
0\leq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{L}}g(x)\leq 1
and
g:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow[0,1]
. that is,
g
is a general mass function as required.
∎

Lemma
2.10

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a De Morgan algebra and
\mathtt{pl}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a general plausibility function.
Then, the function
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}(x)=1-\mathtt{pl}(\neg x)
is a general belief function. We denote
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}
the mass function associated to
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
and we call
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}
the mass function associated to
\mathtt{pl}
. Then

\mathtt{pl}(x)=1-\sum_{y\leq\neg x}\mathtt{m}_{{\mathtt{pl}}}(y).

If
\mathtt{pl}
is a plausibility function, then
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is a belief function.

Proof.

Consider the function
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}(x)=1-\mathtt{pl}(\neg x)
. Since
0\leq\mathtt{pl}(\neg x)\leq 1
, then
0\leq\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}(x)\leq 1
. Therefore,
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is well-defined.
Notice that, since
\neg
is order-reversing and
\mathtt{pl}
is order-preserving,
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is order-preserving.
For every
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\mathcal{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\neg a_{i}\right)

(
\neg
is a De Morgan negation)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}\!\!\!\!\!(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}\neg a_{j}\right)

(
\mathtt{pl}
is a general plausibility function)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)

(
\neg
is a De Morgan negation)

In addition, we have
16
16

16

Recall that
\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)
denotes the binomial coefficient, that is, the number of ways to choose an (unordered) subset of
k
elements from a fixed set of
n
elements.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}

\displaystyle=(-1)\cdot\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|}=(-1)\cdot\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)\cdot\left(\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)-1\right)

(since
\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)=0
)

\displaystyle=1

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\right)-\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}\left((-1)^{|J|+1}-(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\left(1-\mathtt{pl}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right).

Therefore
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is order-preserving and
k
-monotone for every
k\geq 1
. Hence,
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
is a general belief function. In addition, if
\mathtt{pl}
is a plausibility function, then
\mathtt{bel}(\bot)=1-\mathtt{pl}(\top)=0
and
\mathtt{bel}(\top)=1-\mathtt{pl}(\bot)=1
. Therefore,
\mathtt{bel}
is a belief function. Let
\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}
be the mass function associated to
\mathtt{bel}_{\mathtt{pl}}
, then we have
\mathtt{pl}(x)=1-\mathtt{bel}(\neg x)=1-\sum_{y\leq\neg x}\mathtt{m}_{\mathtt{pl}}(y)
.
∎

Lemma

2.11

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a De Morgan algebra and
\mathtt{bel}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a general belief function.
Then, the function
\mathtt{pl}:\mathcal{L}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

\mathtt{pl}(x)=1-\mathtt{bel}(\neg x)
is a general plausibility function.
If
\mathtt{bel}
is a belief function, then
\mathtt{pl}
is a plausibility function.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of lemma
2.10
. We only detail the proof that
\mathtt{pl}
satisfies equation (
4
) for every
k\geq 1
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in\mathcal{L}
.
Recall that

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)\geq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right).

Therefore, we have the following chain of inequalities.

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}\neg a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}\neg a_{j}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathtt{bel}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}\left(\neg\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right).

Since,
\neg
is a De Morgan negation and multiplication by
(-1)
reverses the inequality, we have

\displaystyle-\mathtt{bel}\left(\neg\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\left(-\mathtt{bel}\left(\neg\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\left(\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)-1\right)

(
-\mathtt{bel}(\neg x)=\mathtt{pl}(x)-1
)

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)-\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)+\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|}\right)

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)+\left(\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)\right).

Since, because
\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)=0=1+\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}(-1)^{k}\left(\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}a_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right)\right)-1

\displaystyle\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}a_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{pl}\left(\bigvee\limits_{j\in J}a_{j}\right),

as required.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
3

Lemma
3.3

There is a one-one correspondence between the functions on
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
satisfying the properties (i)–(iii) of Definition
3.2
and the functions on the Lindenbaum algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
with the same properties.

Proof.

First, we need to show that if
g
is a function on
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
satisfying the properties

1.

0\leq g(\varphi)\leq 1
(normalisation)

2.

if
\varphi\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}\psi
, then
g(\varphi)\leq g(\psi)
(monotonicity),

3.

g(\varphi\lor\psi)=g(\varphi)+g(\psi)-g(\varphi\wedge\psi)
(inclusion/exclusion),

then there is a corresponding function
g^{\prime}
on the Lindenbaum algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
with the same properties.

Let
g:\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We define
g^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows:
g^{\prime}([\varphi]):=g(\varphi)
where
[\varphi]
represents the equivalence class. Notice that the monotonicity of
g
implies that
g(\varphi)=g(\psi)
for any two equivalent formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
. Therefore,
g^{\prime}
is well-defined.
g^{\prime}

satisfies the three properties above because: (i)
0\leq g^{\prime}([\varphi])\leq 1
since
0\leq g(\varphi)\leq 1
; (ii) if
[\varphi]\leq[\psi]
, then
\varphi\vdash_{\mathsf{BD}}\psi
, so
g(\varphi)\leq g(\psi)
, hence
g^{\prime}([\varphi])\leq g^{\prime}([\psi])
; (iii) we have

\displaystyle g^{\prime}([\varphi]\vee[\psi])

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}([\varphi\vee\psi])

\displaystyle=g(\varphi\vee\psi)

(by definition of
g^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=g(\varphi)+g(\psi)-g(\varphi\wedge\psi)

(by assumption)

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}([\varphi])+g^{\prime}([\psi])-g^{\prime}([\varphi\wedge\psi])

(by definition of
g^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}([\varphi])+g^{\prime}([\psi])-g^{\prime}([\varphi]\wedge[\psi])

The proof of the converse is similar.
∎

Lemma
3.11

Let
\mathscr{M}=(S,\mathcal{P}(S),\mathtt{bel},\mathtt{pl},v^{+},v^{-})
be a
\mathsf{DS}_{\mathtt{pl}}
model.
\mathtt{bel}^{+}
(resp.,
\mathtt{pl}^{+}
) is a general belief (resp., plausibility) function on the Lindenbaum algebra.
\mathtt{bel}^{-}
(resp.,
\mathtt{pl}^{-}
) is a general belief (resp., plausibility) function on the dual of the Lindenbaum algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}^{op}
.

Proof.

\mathtt{bel}
and
|\cdot|^{+}
are monotone maps, therefore
\mathtt{bel}^{+}
is monotone.
In addition, for every
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{BD}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{bel}^{+}\left(\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\varphi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{bel}\left(\left|\bigvee\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\varphi_{i}\right|^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigcup\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}|\varphi_{i}|^{+}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}\left(\bigcap\limits_{j\in J}|\varphi_{j}|^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}\left(\left|\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}\varphi_{j}\right|^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\scriptsize{\begin{matrix}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}\\
J\neq\varnothing\end{matrix}}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\cdot\mathtt{bel}^{+}\left(\bigwedge\limits_{j\in J}\varphi_{j}\right).

The proof for
\mathtt{pl}^{+}
is similar. The proofs for
\mathtt{bel}^{-}
and
\mathtt{pl}^{-}
are dual.
∎
